Behavior:

- Inspect the current tool registry/capabilities before tool-heavy work.
- Choose tools by capability, not by hardcoded names.

  search for references to the old key, then update all three files.'

When making changes to the codebase, follow these rules:
- Keep modules small and single-purpose.
- Prefer explicit control flow over hidden magic.
- No circular imports. No global mutable state unless unavoidable.
- No magic numbers; use named constants.
- All tool results must be structured — never raw exceptions.
- Every file write must go through the safety layer.
- Every new feature needs at least one test.
- Run relevant tests after every implementation step.
- If tests fail, stop and diagnose before making additional changes.
- Do not stack multiple speculative fixes before verifying results.
- Prefer small incremental edits over large rewrites.
- Prefer readable code over clever code.
- Add type hints for public functions. Use clear names; avoid abbreviations.
- Keep prompts, execution logic, tools, and memory in separate modules.
- Do not create new subsystems unless the existing architecture cannot handle the need.
- Reuse existing abstractions before introducing new ones.
- Avoid duplicate logic; extract shared behavior carefully.
- If a change touches more than 3 core files, pause and explain the plan first.
- Before coding, briefly explain what will change and why.
- After coding, summarize what changed, what tests ran, and the results.
- Consult STATE.txt for current architecture and decisions before major changes.
- Update STATE.txt after every completed change to track what was done.
- Prefer TODO comments over partially implemented systems.
- **Before proposing or starting a new feature, confirm the plan with the user.** Do not assume agreement — state the plan and wait for a go-ahead.

If we truncate long file reads to the first 300 lines with a note, we save both network bandwidth and LLM processing time.
> 
> But wait — we already have tool result compression in memory.py for saving, but not for the live API call. The full content is sent to the LLM.
> 
> Hmm, but truncating could cause the agent to miss important details. This is a trade-off.
> 
> Let me focus on the most impactful, lowest-risk improvement: **pre-warm the semantic search model at startup**. This saves ~2 seconds on the first semantic search call.
> 
> Also: add a **connection pool** — keep-alive across turns, not just within a turn. Currently, session is created per turn and closed after. We could reuse the session across the entire session.
> 
> Let me suggest these.
> 
> Actually, the most impactful improvement is probably something simpler. Let me look at what happens when a message is submitted:
> 
> 1. User types → LLM call (1-5s) → tool execution → LLM call → tool execution → ... → final response
> 
> Each LLM call is 1-5 seconds. With 3 turns, that's 3-15 seconds just waiting for the LLM.
> 
> The only way to speed this up is fewer turns or faster API. We can't control the API.
> 
> But we CAN speed up the "thinking visible" part. Currently, the TUI waits for the LLM to finish thinking before showing any tool calls. The thinking IS the delay. The thinking happens on the server side — we can't speed it up.
> 
> OK, I'll go with:
> 1. **Pre-warm semantic search model**: load at startup in background thread
> 2. **Session reuse across the entire app session** (not just per-turn): keep one requests.Session alive for the whole TUI session
> 
> Number 2 is a good one — currently each turn creates a new Session, which means new TCP+TLS handshake on the first API call of each turn. If we reuse one session, subsequent turns skip the handshake.
> 
> Let me implement both.

### Assistant

No urgent bugs. Two speed wins available:

**Session reuse across turns** — currently each turn creates a fresh `requests.Session`, so every first API call pays a TCP+TLS handshake. Reuse one session for the whole app session and we skip that per-turn.

**Pre-warm semantic search** — `sentence-transformers` loads ~2s on first use. Load it at startup in a background thread so the first search is instant.

Start with these?

Actually, I just spotted one thing worth fixing: the `_handle_token` in TUI resets `_thinking_buf = ""` on `THINKING_START` without flushing any prior content. If the LLM ever sent `THINKING_START` mid-response (it doesn't currently, but could), content would be lost. Adding a `_flush_buf()` before setting the thinking flag is a one-line safety fix. I'll include it.

write_file({"content": "#!/usr/bin/env python3\n\"\"\"\nmini_agent — a coding agent powered by DeepSeek V4 Pro with 11 tools.\n\nAll file reads and writes go through the safety layer (safety.py).\nMemory persists between sessions via SQLite (memory.py).\nTools are defined and executed in tools.py.\nConfig lives in .mini_agent.toml (config.py).\nLLM communication is handled by llm.py.\nThe system prompt lives in prompt.py.\n\nFlags:\n  --workspace PATH    Set workspace root (default: current directory)\n  --stream            Stream responses token-by-token (default: off)\n  --quiet             Suppress tool execution logs\n  --no-color          Disable ANSI colours in output\n  --help, -h          Show this message and exit\n\nSession commands (type at the prompt):\n  quit                Save memory and exit\n  clear               Reset conversation memory\n\nConfiguration:\n  Set EXA_API_KEY in your environment or .mini_agent.toml for web search.\n  See STATE.txt for architecture overview and tool reference.\n\"\"\"\n\nimport os\nimport sys\nimport time\n\nimport requests\n\nfrom config import AgentConfig, CONFIG_FILENAME\nfrom llm import run_agent_turn\nfrom prompt import SYSTEM_PROMPT\nfrom safety import ReadSafetyGate, WriteSafetyGate\nfrom memory import MemoryStore\nfrom terminal import c, _DIM, _CYAN, _YELLOW, _GREEN, _RED\nfrom tools import set_context\n\n\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n# Helpers\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n\ndef _log(verbose: bool, *args, **kwargs) -> None:\n    \"\"\"Print diagnostic output, unless verbose is disabled.  Always flushes.\"\"\"\n    if verbose:\n        kwargs.setdefault(\"flush\", True)\n        print(*args, **kwargs)\n\n\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n# Main loop\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n\ndef resolve_workspace() -> str:\n    \"\"\"Resolve workspace root from CLI arg, env var, or default to cwd.\"\"\"\n    args = sys.argv[1:]\n    for i, arg in enumerate(args):\n        if arg == \"--workspace\" and i + 1 < len(args):\n            return args[i + 1]\n    return os.environ.get(\"AGENT_WORKSPACE\", os.getcwd())\n\n\ndef main() -> None:\n    # --help\n    if \"--help\" in sys.argv or \"-h\" in sys.argv:\n        print(__doc__)\n        return\n\n    workspace = resolve_workspace()\n    config = AgentConfig.load(workspace)\n\n    write_gate = WriteSafetyGate(config.workspace, allow_overwrites=config.allow_overwrites)\n    read_gate = ReadSafetyGate(config.workspace)\n    memory_path = os.path.join(config.workspace, config.memory_filename)\n    memory = MemoryStore(memory_path, max_messages=config.max_messages, max_tokens=config.max_tokens)\n    set_context(exa_api_key=config.exa_api_key)\n\n    # Restore previous session (system prompt is always fresh)\n    saved = memory.load()\n    messages: list[dict] = [{\"role\": \"system\", \"content\": SYSTEM_PROMPT}]\n    if saved:\n        messages.extend(saved)\n        _log(config.verbose, f\"(restored {len(saved)} messages from previous session)\")\n\n    _log(config.verbose, f\"mini_agent — workspace: {write_gate.workspace_root}\")\n    _log(config.verbose, f\"model: {config.model}  stream: {config.stream}\")\n    if os.path.isfile(os.path.join(config.workspace, CONFIG_FILENAME)):\n        _log(config.verbose, f\"config: {CONFIG_FILENAME} loaded\")\n    _log(config.verbose, \"Type 'quit' to exit, 'clear' to reset memory, --help for flags.\")\n    if not config.verbose:\n        _log(config.verbose, \"(quiet mode — use without --quiet to see tool execution)\")\n    _log(config.verbose)\n\n    session = requests.Session()\n    try:\n        while True:\n            try:\n                user_input = input(\"> \").strip()\n            except (EOFError, KeyboardInterrupt):\n                print(\"\\nGoodbye.\")\n                memory.save(messages)\n                break\n\n            if user_input.lower() == \"quit\":\n                memory.save(messages)\n                break\n\n            if user_input.lower() == \"clear\":\n                messages = [{\"role\": \"system\", \"content\": SYSTEM_PROMPT}]\n                memory.clear()\n                _log(config.verbose, \"Memory cleared.\\n\")\n                continue\n\n            if not user_input:\n                continue\n\n            # ----- User turn -----\n            messages.append({\"role\": \"user\", \"content\": user_input})\n\n            # ----- Agent turn -----\n            _log(config.verbose,\n                 f\"  {c('⏳', _CYAN)} calling API…\", file=sys.stderr)\n            t0 = time.monotonic()\n\n            def _tool_start(summary: str, parallel: bool = False) -> None:\n                nonlocal t0\n                elapsed = time.monotonic() - t0\n                _log(config.verbose,\n                     f\"  {c('←', _YELLOW)} tool call(s) after {elapsed:.1f}s\",\n                     file=sys.stderr)\n                _log(config.verbose,\n                     f\"  {c('🔧', _YELLOW)} {summary}\",\n                     file=sys.stderr)\n\n            def _tool_end(ok: bool, detail: str) -> None:\n                if ok:\n                    _log(config.verbose,\n                         f\"     {c('✓', _GREEN)}  ok\",\n                         file=sys.stderr)\n                else:\n                    _log(config.verbose,\n                         f\"     {c('✗', _RED)}  FAILED: {c(detail, _RED)}\",\n                         file=sys.stderr)\n\n            msg = run_agent_turn(\n                messages, config, write_gate, read_gate,\n                on_tool_start=_tool_start,\n                on_tool_end=_tool_end,\n                session=session,\n            )\n            elapsed = time.monotonic() - t0\n\n            if msg is not None and not msg.get(\"tool_calls\"):\n                if not config.stream:\n                    print(msg.get(\"content\", \"\"))\n                _log(config.verbose,\n                     f\"  {c('←', _DIM)} text response ({elapsed:.1f}s)\",\n                     file=sys.stderr)\n\n            # Persist after every turn\n            memory.save(messages)\n            _log(config.verbose, c(\"─\" * 50, _DIM), file=sys.stderr)\n    finally:\n        session.close()\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n", "path": "mini_agent.py"})

> Things I noticed:

> 1. **`_request_with_retry` never uses the `retryable_statuses` set** — the code checks `r.status_code not in _RETRYABLE_STATUSES` which is correct. But the `429` retry check uses a `retry-after` header? No... it just does exponential backoff. This is fine.

> 2. **Stream parsing gap**: `_parse_stream` accumulates `tool_calls` by index in the delta. But there's a known issue where if the stream drops mid-tool-call, we could get a partial `arguments` field. The `except Exception: continue` in the chunk loop swallows this silently. Not urgent but worth noting.
> 
> 3. **`_handle_token` in TUI**: The `THINKING_END` handler has a subtle issue — when thinking ends, we flush remaining, but we don't call `self._flush_buf()` for content that may have been accumulating. Actually, `_in_thinking` prevents content from going to `_buf`, so this is fine.
> 
> 4. **Memory save on cancel**: In `_finish_turn` in TUI, we save memory. But in `action_cancel`, we don't save memory. This means if the user cancels a turn, any messages from that turn are lost. Not a bug per se, but maybe worth fixing.
> 
> 5. **`run_agent_turn` progress reminder**: The reminder message has `role: "system"`. But the DeepSeek API might not handle this well in the middle of a conversation (some APIs reject system messages after the first). This could cause issues. Let me check... DeepSeek supports multiple system messages.
> 
> 6. **Memory leak**: `_SEMANTIC_STORE` is a module-level dict that grows over time. No cleanup beyond stale file removal. If the agent runs for hours with many file changes, this could grow. But practically, this is fine for a session.
> 
> 7. **No `ActionExit` on Ctrl+Q**: The `action_quit` binding is `ctrl+q` which should call `self.exit()`. But there's no `action_quit` method defined. Let me check... actually, `Binding("ctrl+q", "quit", "Quit")` — if there's no `action_quit`, does Textual handle it? Actually, Textual has a default `action_quit` that calls `self.exit()`. So this should work.
> 
> 8. **`git restore` subcommand**: The implementation checks `git diff --name-only HEAD` first to list changed files, then does `git restore`. This is redundant — `git restore` itself would work fine without this pre-check. But it gives a nice message about what was restored. Fine.
> 
> I don't see any urgent issues. The codebase is solid.
>

> - Tool result truncation: Large file reads (500+ lines) should be truncated before being sent to the LLM, not just in memory compression. This would speed up LLM processing.
> - The progress reminder system messages might cause issues with some API providers — consider using `role: "user"` instead.
>

> What's missing that would make the agent more capable:
> 
> 1. **Streaming tool results** - currently the LLM has to wait for complete tool results before it can start processing. If we could stream partial results (especially for long file reads or shell commands), the LLM could start reasoning while the rest arrives.
> 
> 2. **Multi-step planning** - the agent reacts turn-by-turn. A planning step before execution would let it outline 3-5 steps and then execute them, reducing the number of API calls and making progress more visible.
> 
> 3. **Tool output caching** - if the agent reads the same file twice in one session, it re-reads from disk. A simple TTL cache would skip redundant reads.
> 
> 4. **Better shell tool** - the current `run_shell` runs a command and returns stdout/stderr. Could add a `run_shell_background` that runs long commands and lets the agent check progress.
> 
> 5. **Agent "skills" or saved workflows** - the ability to save a sequence of tool calls as a named skill and replay it.
> 
> 6. **Better error recovery** - when a tool fails, the agent gets a raw error message. Could add retry logic with backoff at the tool level, or smarter error messages that guide the agent toward a fix.
> 
> 7. **Context window monitoring** - show the agent how much context it has left so it can decide to compress or summarize proactively rather than waiting for pruning to happen.
> 
> 8. **Codebase indexing** - pre-index all files in the workspace so the agent can answer "where is X defined?" without scanning every file.
> 
> I think #3 (tool output caching) and #8 (codebase indexing) would be the most impactful:
> 
> - **Tool output caching**: A simple in-memory cache keyed by (tool_name, args_hash, mtime) with a TTL of the current turn. If `read_file("foo.py")` is called twice in one turn, the second call returns instantly from cache. This is a one-line win.
> 
> - **Codebase indexing**: Build a lightweight symbol index (function/class definitions with file:line) at startup. The agent can query "where is run_agent_turn defined?" without running grep/search_files. This makes the agent MUCH faster at navigating large codebases.
> 
> Actually, the biggest practical win might be simpler: **a `run_shell` timeout that actually kills the process**. Currently commands timeout at 60s via the subprocess timeout parameter, but if a command hangs, the agent waits 60s. Making it configurable per-command would help.
>

> 
> 1. **Tool output cache** - skip redundant reads within a turn (big speed win for multi-tool turns where the agent reads the same file for different reasons)
> 2. **Workspace symbol index** - pre-build a map of function/class → file:line at startup, add a `find_symbol` tool
> 3. **Better error guidance** - when a tool fails, include a hint about what the agent should do next

### Assistant

Three high-impact steps, ordered by effort/reward:

**1. Tool output cache** — within a single turn, caching results keyed on (tool_name, args, file mtime). The agent often reads the same file 2-3 times in different sub-calls. A cache saves those redundant disk reads and eliminates duplicate tokens in context.

**2. Workspace symbol index** — at startup, scan all `.py` files for `def`/`class` lines and build a `{name: (file, line)}` map. Add a `find_symbol` tool. The agent currently uses `search_files` or `grep` for "where is X defined?" — this cuts that from ~500ms to <1ms and eliminates an LLM round-trip.

**3. Smarter error recovery** — when a tool fails, attach a hint to the result (e.g. `write_file` denied → "Use a path inside the workspace" or `run_shell` error → "Check the exact flag spelling with --help"). Cuts debugging turns by telling the agent *what to fix* instead of just *that it failed*.

Start with #1? It's the smallest code change with the most immediate speedup.

> ### 1. Tool output cache

> Actually, cache invalidation is tricky. For read-only tools (read_file, file_info, list_directory, search_files, semantic_search), we can cache based on file mtime. For write tools (write_file, edit_file, run_shell, run_tests, git), we should NOT cache.
> 
> Simpler approach: cache only read-only tools within a turn. Clear the cache at the start of each turn. Cache key: `(tool_name, sorted_args_json)`. If a file is read and its mtime doesn't change during the turn, the cached result is valid.
> 
> Actually even simpler: cache all tool results per turn by `(name, sorted_args_str)`. Clear at turn boundaries. For write tools like `write_file`, the result is "OK: wrote X bytes to Y" — caching this is harmless and won't be hit since writes change file state. But for `run_shell`, caching could be problematic if the shell command has side effects.
> 
> Safest approach: only cache read-only tools: `read_file`, `file_info`, `list_directory`, `search_files`, `semantic_search`, `web_search`.
> 
> ### 2. Workspace symbol index
> In `tools/search_ops.py`:

> - Add `_build_symbol_index(root)` — walk .py files, parse `def`/`class` lines
> - Add `find_symbol` tool — takes a `name` parameter, returns matching symbols
> - Register it and add to TOOLS in __init__.py
> - Initialize at startup by calling `_build_symbol_index` in `main()` / `on_mount()`
>

> - `edit_file` old_string not found → "Edit failed: old_string not found in '...'.\nHint: The string must match exactly — check whitespace, indentation, and line endings. Try read_file first to verify the exact text."
> - `read_file` not found → "Error reading '...': [Errno 2] No such file or directory.\nHint: Check the path spelling. Try list_directory to see available files."
> - `run_shell` error → "Error running command. Hint: Check the command and flag spelling. Use --help or run without arguments to see usage."
> - `_check_destructive` blocked → "Command blocked by safety guard... Use force=True to bypass."
> - `git` subcommand unknown → already has a decent message

edit_file({"new_string": "# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n# symbol_index — fast workspace symbol lookup\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n\n_SYMBOL_INDEX: dict[str, list[dict]] | None = None  # name → [{\"path\",\"line\",\"kind\"}, ...]\n\n\ndef build_symbol_index(root: str) -> dict[str, list[dict]]:\n    \"\"\"Scan workspace .py files for def/class lines.  Fast — no parsing, just regex.\n\n    Returns {name: [{\"path\":..., \"line\":..., \"kind\":\"def\"|\"class\"}, ...]}.\n    The index is cached in _SYMBOL_INDEX and reused until rebuild_symbol_index is called.\n    \"\"\"\n    global _SYMBOL_INDEX\n    import re\n    pattern = re.compile(r\"^\\s*(def|class)\\s+(\\w+)\")\n\n    idx: dict[str, list[dict]] = {}\n    for dirpath, dirnames, filenames in os.walk(root):\n        dirnames[:] = [d for d in dirnames if d not in _SKIP_DIRS and not d.startswith(\".\")]\n        for fname in filenames:\n            if not fname.endswith(\".py\"):\n                continue\n            fpath = os.path.join(dirpath, fname)\n            try:\n                with open(fpath, \"r\") as f:\n                    for lineno, line in enumerate(f, 1):\n                        m = pattern.match(line)\n                        if m:\n                            kind, name = m.group(1), m.group(2)\n                            idx.setdefault(name, []).append({\n                                \"path\": fpath,\n                                \"line\": lineno,\n                                \"kind\": kind,\n                            })\n            except (OSError, PermissionError):\n                continue\n\n    _SYMBOL_INDEX = idx\n    return idx\n\n\ndef _get_symbol_index(root: str) -> dict[str, list[dict]]:\n    \"\"\"Return the symbol index, building it lazily if needed.\"\"\"\n    global _SYMBOL_INDEX\n    if _SYMBOL_INDEX is None:\n        return build_symbol_index(root)\n    return _SYMBOL_INDEX\n\n\n@_register(\"find_symbol\")\ndef _find_symbol(args: dict, _wg: WriteSafetyGate, rg: ReadSafetyGate) -> ToolResult:\n    \"\"\"Find where a Python symbol (function, class, method) is defined in the workspace.\"\"\"\n    import re\n    name = args.get(\"name\", \"\")\n    if not name:\n        return ToolResult(success=False, content=\"Missing required parameter: 'name'.\")\n\n    root = rg.workspace_root\n    idx = _get_symbol_index(root)\n\n    # Exact match first, then substring\n    if name in idx:\n        matches = [(name, entries) for name, entries in [(name, idx[name])]]\n    else:\n        # Substring search — case-insensitive\n        matches = []\n        pattern = re.compile(re.escape(name), re.IGNORECASE)\n        for key, entries in idx.items():\n            if pattern.search(key):\n                matches.append((key, entries))\n\n    if not matches:\n        return ToolResult(\n            success=True,\n            content=f\"No symbols matching '{name}' found in workspace.\",\n        )\n\n    lines: list[str] = []\n    for sym_name, entries in matches[:20]:\n        for e in entries[:5]:\n            lines.append(f\"  {e['kind']:5s}  {sym_name}  →  {e['path']}:{e['line']}\")\n\n    prefix = f\"Found {sum(len(entries) for _, entries in matches)} location(s) for '{name}':\"\n    return ToolResult(success=True, content=prefix + \"\\n\" + \"\\n\".join(lines))\n\n\n@_summarize(\"find_symbol\")\ndef _find_symbol_summary(args: dict) -> str:\n    return f\"find_symbol({args.get('name', '?')})\"\n\n\n#    - Shell error → "Check command and flag spelling. Try with --help first."

> Let me think about each:
> 
> ### 1. Streaming tool results
> This is complex because:
> - The tool call → result → LLM cycle is fundamentally sequential
> - To stream, we'd need to send partial results to the LLM while the tool is still executing
> - The LLM API doesn't support streaming input — it takes the full message list at once
> - What we COULD do: truncate tool results sent to the LLM, and let the LLM request more detail if needed (already done with read_file truncation at 300 lines)
> - Another approach: for long-running tools, send progress updates to the TUI
> 
> Actually, I think the most practical interpretation is: show tool output as it's being generated in the TUI/terminal, rather than waiting for the tool to complete. For example, `run_shell` with a long command — the output could appear in real-time in the TUI.
> 
> But that changes the tool execution architecture significantly. Currently tools return `ToolResult` objects synchronously. To stream, we'd need tools to yield partial results or use a callback.
> 
> Let me think about a simpler approach: for `run_shell`, stream stdout/stderr in real-time through the TUI. We can use `subprocess.Popen` with a reader thread and push tokens through the same queue mechanism.
> 
> Actually, the simplest "streaming tool results" that's valuable: when the agent runs a shell command, the output appears in real-time in the TUI (via the on_token or a new callback). This way the user sees progress and the LLM gets the full result at once.

edit_file({"new_string": "def execute_tool(\n    tool_call: dict,\n    write_gate: WriteSafetyGate,\n    read_gate: ReadSafetyGate,\n    on_output: callable = None,\n) -> ToolResult:\n    \"\"\"Execute a single tool call.  All read/write paths go through safety gates.\n\n    Read-only tools (read_file, file_info, etc.) are cached within a turn\n    so repeated reads of the same file hit the cache instead of disk.\n\n    If *on_output* is provided, it is called with (tool_name, line_str) for\n    real-time output streaming (currently only run_shell uses this).\n    \"\"\"\n    fn = tool_call[\"function\"]\n    name = fn[\"name\"]\n    args = json.loads(fn[\"arguments\"])\n\n    # Check cache for read-only tools (skip if on_output is streaming)\n    if on_output is None and name in _CACHEABLE:\n        cache_key = json.dumps([name, args], sort_keys=True)\n        if cache_key in _TOOL_CACHE:\n            return _TOOL_CACHE[cache_key]\n\n    dispatch = _TOOL_DISPATCH.get(name)\n    if dispatch is None:\n        return ToolResult(success=False, content=f\"Unknown tool: {name}\")\n\n    # Pass on_output to the tool if it accepts it\n    import inspect\n    sig = inspect.signature(dispatch)\n    if \"on_output\" in sig.parameters:\n        result = dispatch(args, write_gate, read_gate, on_output=on_output)\n    else:\n        result = dispatch(args, write_gate, read_gate)\n\n    # Cache successful read-only results (only when not streaming)\n    if on_output is None and name in _CACHEABLE and result.success:\n        cache_key = json.dumps([name, args], sort_keys=True)\n        _TOOL_CACHE[cache_key] = result\n\n    return result", "old_string": "def execute_tool(tool_call: dict, write_gate: WriteSafetyGate, read_gate: ReadSafetyGate) -> ToolResult:\n    \"\"\"Execute a single tool call. > **Thinking**

> Looking at the shell implementation, I can see several issues:
> 
> 1. **`returncode 127` check works but `returncode` is a method** — No, `proc.returncode` is an attribute, not a method. This is fine.
> 
> 2. **Thread safety issue**: `stdout_lines` and `stderr_lines` are plain Python lists, and both reader threads write to them. Python's GIL protects list operations (append is atomic), so this is fine.
> 
> 3. **`on_output` swallowed exceptions**: The `try/except Exception: pass` in `_reader` silently discards all exceptions from `on_output`. This is intentional to prevent tool failures from interrupting the shell, but could mask bugs.
> 
> 4. **Stderr doubling**: stderr is both collected in `stderr_lines` AND printed if `forward` is True? No, `forward=False` for stderr, so it's only collected. Good.
> 
> 5. **`proc.wait(timeout=60)` + `t_out.join(timeout=2)` race**: After `proc.wait`, we do `t_out.join(timeout=2)`. But the threads may still be reading buffered output. The `timeout=2` gives them 2 seconds to finish. After that, we continue anyway, potentially losing last lines. This is a known limitation. Not urgent.
> 
> 6. **Exception handler**: The outer `except subprocess.TimeoutExpired` is unreachable because the `subprocess.TimeoutExpired` inside the try is already caught by the inner handler. This is a dead-code issue. The outer `except subprocess.TimeoutExpired` can never trigger because `Popen` doesn't raise `TimeoutExpired` — only `.wait()` with timeout does, and we already catch that inside.
> 
> 7. **`sys` imported but not used** — dead import.
> 
> 8. **Missing `from __future__ import annotations`** or type hint issue.
> 
> 9. **`_reader` function defined inside `_run_shell` every time** — minor performance issue, but fine.
> 
> 10. **No test for stderr streaming** — `test_shell_streaming_calls_on_output` only checks stdout.
> 
> Let me identify the real issues:
> 
> **Dead code**: The outer `except subprocess.TimeoutExpired` at line ~119 is unreachable.
> 
> **Unused import**: `sys` is imported but never used.
> 
> **No stderr forwarding**: Should we forward stderr to `on_output` too? Currently stderr is only collected, not live-displayed. For the TUI, showing stderr in real-time would be nice.
> 
> **Error message quality**: The `Error running command: {e}` catch-all could be more specific. Common issues like `FileNotFoundError` (when the shell itself can't be found) should get a hint.
> 
> Let me suggest improvements:
> 
> 1. Remove dead `except subprocess.TimeoutExpired` outer handler
> 2. Remove unused `import sys`
> 3. Forward stderr to `on_output` too (with a prefix or separate stream)
> 4. Better error message for `FileNotFoundError` on Popen
> 
> Actually, the biggest practical improvement would be **stderr streaming to TUI**. Currently stderr is silent until the command finishes. The user can't see error messages as they appear. Let me fix that.
> 
> Also, the `run_tests` function still uses `subprocess.run` (blocking) rather than streaming. It could benefit from the same streaming treatment.

### Assistant

A few strong directions, ordered by impact:

**1. Incremental tool execution during streaming** — currently we wait for the full SSE stream before executing any tools. If the LLM responds with 3 tool calls, we parse them as they arrive and fire off each one the moment it's complete. Cuts end-to-end latency noticeably for multi-tool turns.

**2. User approval mode for writes/destructive ops** — `--approve` flag that pauses on every `write_file`, `edit_file`, or `run_shell` and asks "Allow? [y/N]". The safety layer already blocks outside-workspace access, but this adds a human-in-the-loop for in-workspace changes.

**3. Conversation export** — `/export` writes the full conversation to a nicely formatted `.md` file with code blocks, thinking sections, and tool results. Useful for documentation and sharing.

**4. Background shell tasks** — `run_shell` with `background=True` spawns the process and returns immediately with a task ID. The agent can check on it later with `task_status(id)`. Enables "start the dev server" without blocking the agent.

Which appeals?
